I mean… yes, that’s how it feels to me. Not everybody at work, but my supervisor and a couple of people around her, I feel like they’re watching me different than they watch other people.\n\nEven when I’m calm, not yelling, not upset, just doing my job, I still get this strong sense that they’re keeping track of me. Like little notes, little things I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer questions. And then they make these comments that sound normal to other people, but to me they don’t feel normal. They’ll say things about being careful, or making sure residents are safe, and it comes right when I’m there, right after I did something, like they’re trying to see how I’ll react.\n\nSo yeah, I do feel specifically targeted. Like they’re trying to build something on me, trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that really bothers me because I care about them. I take that part seriously. I’m not trying to hurt anybody or be careless. But it feels like they already decided something about me and now they’re just collecting little pieces to make it look true.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that things you see or hear on TV, or read in the newspaper, are specifically about you or related to you in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really TV or newspapers, no… I don’t usually feel like the news is talking about me or anything like that. It’s more what happens at work, with people right there.\n\nLike even when I’m calm and just doing my job, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple of people there are keeping notes on me, or watching me closer than everybody else. And they’ll make these little comments that sound normal, I guess, like about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, but it feels like they’re saying it for me to hear. Like they’re testing how I react.\n\nSo it’s not like I see something on TV and think, “That’s about me.” It’s more the comments and looks at work. It feels connected to me, like they’re trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors, and I don’t think other people see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble at work or in your personal life for doing something that hurt someone else, but you didn't think it was a big deal at the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, not in the way you’re saying it, I don’t think. I don’t remember some big thing where I hurt somebody and thought, “Oh, that’s nothing,” and then later everybody said it was serious.\n\nAt work, I try to be careful with people. Especially with the seniors, because they’re not always steady, and you have to think about how you move them, how you talk to them, all that. So if someone says I did something wrong, I usually take that seriously, even if I don’t agree with how they’re saying it.\n\nIn my personal life… I don’t know. I’ve had arguments, like anybody. Maybe I’ve said things too harsh or came off stronger than I meant to, but I don’t know if I’d call that hurting someone and not thinking it mattered. I can get defensive if I feel like someone’s accusing me, but I don’t set out to hurt people. If you mean physically or doing something dangerous and then brushing it off, no, I can’t really think of anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your supervisor and coworkers are targeting you is something that might not be true, or do you feel absolutely certain about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if there’s a chance I’m wrong, and I guess in my head I can say, like, maybe I don’t have a paper in my hand proving it. I can’t show you a notebook where they wrote my name down or anything like that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a maybe to me. It feels pretty certain. Even when I’m calm, even when I’m not upset and I’m just doing the work normal, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple people around her are keeping track of me. Like they’re watching what I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer things, and then these little comments come right after. They’ll say something about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, and it sounds innocent if you just hear the words, but the timing of it… it feels like it’s aimed at me.\n\nSo I don’t feel absolutely certain like I’ve got hard proof, but inside I feel sure enough that it’s hard to shake. It feels like they’re testing me, trying to get a reaction or trying to build something to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that’s what bothers me, because I do care about them. I don’t think I’m imagining the way they’re acting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you consistently disregard others' feelings or needs in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it to me like that, like, “You consistently disregard people’s feelings or needs.” That sounds pretty strong. I mean, people have told me I can be defensive, or that I’m not hearing them right away when I feel accused, and I can see how maybe that comes off like I don’t care in the moment. But I don’t think I’m somebody who just ignores what other people need.\n\nAt work especially, the whole job is about paying attention to people’s needs, you know? If someone needs help getting up, or they’re uncomfortable, or they’re scared, you have to notice that. So if somebody told me I didn’t care about that, I’d have a hard time accepting it, because I do try.\n\nIn my personal life, I guess I’ve had arguments where someone felt like I wasn’t listening enough, or I was too focused on defending myself. But consistently disregarding feelings… no, I haven’t really been told that, not in that way. If there’s something specific you mean, I might be able to answer better.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others, even before the issues started at your current job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “always.” I mean, I’ve had problems getting along with certain people, sure, but I don’t think it’s been my whole life like I just can’t be around people or I’m always fighting with everybody.\n\nBefore this job, I had disagreements, like anyone does. Sometimes I don’t explain myself right, or I get frustrated if I feel like somebody’s putting words in my mouth. And I can be quiet around people if I don’t trust the situation, so maybe some people take that the wrong way. But I’ve also gotten along with people fine. I’ve had coworkers I could talk to, people I helped, people who knew I was trying to do my job.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had difficulty getting along with others. Not like this. This current job feels different to me. But if you mean have I ever had conflicts before, yeah, I mean, I’m 41, I’ve had conflicts. I just don’t think it was some pattern where everywhere I went people had a problem with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're more important or special than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like “more important than others,” because that sounds like I think I’m better than everybody, and I don’t really mean it that way. I know everybody has their role, and people have degrees and titles and all that.\n\nBut… yeah, sometimes I do get this strong feeling like I see things clearer than they do, especially when it comes to keeping the seniors safe and actually caring about them like people. Like the supervisors and the people with the badges or titles, they talk like they know everything, but then I’m watching what actually happens on the floor, and I’m thinking, you’re missing the whole point. You’re not seeing what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s not even just that I’m good at my job. It feels bigger than that sometimes. Like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was somehow picked, or meant, to be the one who understands what the senior center really needs. I know that probably sounds like I’m making myself sound special, and maybe that’s what you’re asking, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like ego. It feels like responsibility. Like I’m the one noticing the things everyone else is ignoring.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do feel that way sometimes. Not like every single thing I say is more valuable than everyone else’s, but with certain things, especially at the senior center, I do feel like I understand what’s really going on better than the people who are supposed to be in charge.\n\nLike, they have the titles, they have the paperwork, they have the meetings and all that, but I’m there with the old folks, you know? I’m seeing when somebody’s not being watched right, or when somebody’s scared, or when the way they’re doing things isn’t really caring for them like human beings. And then when I try to say something, it feels like they brush it off because I’m “just” the aide or because I don’t have the right position.\n\nSo yes, in those moments, I do feel like my opinion should carry more weight than it does. Sometimes it feels like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was meant to see it, or picked somehow to understand what the place needs in a way they don’t. I know how that sounds, like I’m saying I’m above people, but that’s not how I mean it. It feels more like… I’m the one actually paying attention, and they’re missing what’s right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special destiny or role in life that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it a “destiny” exactly, because that sounds kind of big, like I’m saying I was put on earth for some grand mission or something. But I do get that feeling sometimes, especially when it comes to the senior center and the old folks there.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be watching how things are run, and I’ll see stuff that the people with titles don’t seem to notice. They’ll have their rules and clipboards and meetings, but I’m the one actually seeing the residents, seeing what they need, seeing what doesn’t feel safe or right. And then I get this strong feeling like I understand it better than they do. Like not just “I have an opinion,” but like I’m supposed to be the one to step in and fix it.\n\nSometimes it feels like I was picked for that somehow. Like maybe I’m there for a reason, to protect them or to make people listen. If they would just stop brushing me off and actually hear me, I feel like I could fix the whole place, or at least a lot of what’s wrong with it.\n\nI know saying that probably sounds like I think I’m special or above other people, but it doesn’t feel like that inside. It feels more like pressure, like responsibility. Like I’m seeing something important and everybody else is walking past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special connection or insight that others lack, which sets you apart and makes your thoughts or actions more significant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know I keep coming back to the senior center, but that’s where it comes up the strongest for me. It’s like I can walk into a situation there and just feel what’s off, you know? Like when the residents aren’t being watched right, or when somebody’s acting scared or confused and everyone else is just treating it like routine. I’ll see it, and it feels obvious to me, but the people with the titles, the supervisors, the ones who are supposed to know better, they don’t seem to catch it.\n\nAnd then I start feeling like… why am I the only one seeing this? Like there’s something about me that understands safety and caring for old folks in a way they don’t. Not because I went to some big school or have some fancy certificate, because I don’t, but because I’m actually there with them and I pay attention. I notice the little things. I know when something doesn’t sit right.\n\nSometimes it does feel like a special kind of insight, I guess. Like I was put there, or picked somehow, to fix what’s wrong in that place. Like if they would just listen to me instead of brushing me off, I could straighten a lot of it out. Maybe even the whole place, honestly. I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels real. It feels like I’m carrying something important and they’re ignoring it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about your supervisor and coworkers targeting you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say yes, because I know that’s probably the reasonable answer, right? Like if somebody showed me something clear enough, I should be able to say, “Okay, maybe I got it wrong.” I’m not trying to be impossible.\n\nBut honestly, even when I’m calm, even when I’m not worked up or angry about it, I still feel pretty sure my supervisor and a couple people there are keeping notes on me. Not just normal work notes, I mean like watching me for little mistakes, writing down how I talk to the seniors, how I react to things, whether I seem frustrated or not. And then they make these little comments that sound casual, but they don’t feel casual to me. It feels like they’re testing me, like they’re trying to see if I’ll slip up or prove I’m not safe to be around the residents.\n\nSo if you ask what would change my mind… I don’t know. If they came right out and showed me there weren’t any notes, or explained every comment, maybe that would help some. But even then, part of me would wonder if they just hid it better or knew what to say. Because it’s not just one thing. It’s a bunch of little things that add up, the looks, the timing, the way they say stuff when I walk by.\n\nI know that makes it hard, because it probably sounds like there’s no answer that would satisfy me. I’m not saying nothing could ever change my mind, but right now it feels very real to me. It feels like they’ve already decided something about me and they’re just trying to collect enough to make it stick.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever used your position or influence to take advantage of someone else for personal gain?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, when you say “take advantage,” that sounds like using somebody or manipulating them, and I don’t see myself that way.\n\nAt the senior center, I might speak up a lot, probably more than they want me to. And I might push when I think something isn’t right, but that’s not for me to get something out of it. I’m not trying to get money from anybody or get special favors or make someone do something for me. If anything, I feel like I’m usually the one getting in trouble for opening my mouth.\n\nI mean, I guess I’ve asked people to listen to me, or I’ve tried to get residents’ concerns taken seriously, but that’s different from personal gain. I’m not trying to use my position to benefit myself. I don’t really have that kind of power anyway. I’m just an aide. So… no, not that I can think of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, I know I keep coming back to the senior center, but that’s where it comes up the strongest for me. It’s like I can walk into a situation there and just feel what’s off, you know? I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels real. It feels like I’m carrying something important and they’re ignoring it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about your supervisor and coworkers targeting you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say yes, because I know that’s probably the reasonable answer, right? And then they make these little comments that sound casual, but they don’t feel casual to me. It’s a bunch of little things that add up, the looks, the timing, the way they say stuff when I walk by.\n\nI know that makes it hard, because it probably sounds like there’s no answer that would satisfy me. I’m not saying nothing could ever change my mind, but right now it feels very real to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do feel that way, if I’m being honest. I know it probably sounds like I’m putting myself above everybody, and I don’t mean it like I’m perfect or I know every rule in some textbook way. But when it comes to actually seeing what the seniors need, what makes them safe, what makes them feel cared for, I really do feel like I understand it better than a lot of the people who have the titles.

Like the supervisors, the ones who are supposed to be in charge, they’ll look at a situation and treat it like paperwork or procedure. But I’ll look at the same thing and think, “No, you’re missing what’s really happening here.” I notice when somebody is scared, when they’re not being watched closely enough, when something could go wrong before it goes wrong. And then when nobody listens, it makes me feel like… why am I seeing this so clearly if I’m not supposed to do something about it?

So yeah, sometimes it feels bigger than just a job. It feels like I was put there for a reason, like maybe I’m the one who’s supposed to fix the place, or at least make people wake up and do right by the seniors. I’ve had moments where I think, if they would just stop brushing me off and actually listen, I could straighten out the whole senior center. Not just one little problem, but the way they run things.

I know that sounds like a lot. I hear myself saying it and I know it might sound extreme. But in my head, when I’m there and I’m seeing things happen, it feels true. It feels like I have a role there that other people don’t understand.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, um… I think what finally got me to come in was something that happened at work. I’m a personal care aide, so we have these staff meetings, and they were talking about fall prevention, you know, how to keep people from slipping or getting hurt, and I just… I got really worked up.\n\nI don’t mean I just raised my voice a little. I mean I stood up and started kind of acting out the whole thing, like a drill, moving chairs around and waving my arms, showing how somebody might fall and how we should catch it or stop it. In my head it felt like I was making a point, like everyone needed to see it, not just talk about it. But people were telling me to sit down, and I could hear them, but I couldn’t seem to stop right away. I just kept going with it.\n\nIt was embarrassing after, because I know that’s not how you’re supposed to act in a staff meeting. That’s mostly why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Has this kind of thing happened to you before, where you got really worked up and acted out like that?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s the thing, it’s not like I planned it or anything. I got so worked up in the staff meeting that I started acting out the whole fall-prevention thing, like it was a drill, moving chairs and waving my arms around, showing how somebody could fall and how we should react.\n\nAnd even when people were telling me, “Sit down, sit down,” I heard them, but I couldn’t really make myself stop right away. It felt like I had to finish showing what I meant, like if I stopped then nobody would understand it the right way. So… yeah, that’s the kind of thing I’m talking about. It’s happened like that where I get caught up and I’m doing too much before I realize how it looks.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been like this, or is this something that has developed over time?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve always been like this. I mean, I’ve always had my ways, I guess, like anybody does, but this kind of getting so wound up and feeling like I have to push my point or act it out… that’s more recent.\n\nIt’s been going on maybe about a year and five months, something like that. I can kind of trace it back to when the new supervisor started. Things changed after that. She started criticizing my work more, and not just little suggestions — it felt like she was watching me, picking apart how I did things. And before that, I used to lead some of the briefings, you know, I had a role, people listened to me. Then those got taken away from me, and I think that really did something to me.\n\nSo no, it developed over time. It wasn’t like I woke up one day different, but since all that started, I’ve been getting more worked up about things at work, especially when I feel like people aren’t hearing me or they’re taking something away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that you think are true but others around you don't believe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. And I know how it probably sounds when I say it out loud, but it doesn’t feel like just me being sensitive.\n\nEven when I’m calm, like not in the middle of an argument or anything, I still feel pretty sure my supervisor and a couple people at work are keeping notes on me. Not official notes maybe, I don’t know, but tracking little things I say and do. And they’ll make these little comments, like small comments that other people might say are nothing, but to me it feels like they’re testing me.\n\nLike they’ll say something about “being careful” or “making sure residents are safe,” and it feels aimed at me, like they’re trying to see how I react, or trying to build some kind of proof that I’m not safe around the seniors. That’s the part that bothers me, because I care about them. I do that work because I care. But I feel like they’re trying to make it look like I shouldn’t be trusted with them.\n\nOther people don’t really believe me when I say that. They’ll say, “Maybe they’re just talking generally,” or “Maybe you’re reading into it,” but I don’t think I am. I really don’t. It feels too pointed, too repeated, like there’s a reason behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people at work are actually plotting against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “harm” like physically hurt me, no… I’m not saying they’re going to jump me in the parking lot or something. But plotting, in the sense of trying to build a case against me? Yeah, that’s what it feels like.\n\nEven when I’m calm, when I’m just doing my shift and trying to focus, I still feel like my supervisor and maybe one or two others are keeping track of me. Like writing down little things, watching how I answer, watching how I move around the residents, waiting for me to mess up. And then the comments they make — they sound normal to everybody else, I guess, but to me they feel aimed right at me. Like, “We need to make sure everyone is safe,” or “Be careful with how you handle that,” and it’s like… why say it right then, right when I’m there?\n\nSo I don’t know if they’re trying to harm me exactly, but I do think they’re trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors, like they want people to stop trusting me. And that bothers me a lot, because I care about those people. I’m not careless with them. But it feels like they’re testing me and gathering things to make me look that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people at work are specifically targeting you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s how it feels to me. Not everybody at work, but my supervisor and a couple of people around her, I feel like they’re watching me different than they watch other people.\n\nEven when I’m calm, not yelling, not upset, just doing my job, I still get this strong sense that they’re keeping track of me. Like little notes, little things I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer questions. And then they make these comments that sound normal to other people, but to me they don’t feel normal. They’ll say things about being careful, or making sure residents are safe, and it comes right when I’m there, right after I did something, like they’re trying to see how I’ll react.\n\nSo yeah, I do feel specifically targeted. Like they’re trying to build something on me, trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that really bothers me because I care about them. I take that part seriously. I’m not trying to hurt anybody or be careless. But it feels like they already decided something about me and now they’re just collecting little pieces to make it look true.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that things you see or hear on TV, or read in the newspaper, are specifically about you or related to you in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really TV or newspapers, no… I don’t usually feel like the news is talking about me or anything like that. It’s more what happens at work, with people right there.\n\nLike even when I’m calm and just doing my job, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple of people there are keeping notes on me, or watching me closer than everybody else. And they’ll make these little comments that sound normal, I guess, like about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, but it feels like they’re saying it for me to hear. Like they’re testing how I react.\n\nSo it’s not like I see something on TV and think, “That’s about me.” It’s more the comments and looks at work. It feels connected to me, like they’re trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors, and I don’t think other people see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble at work or in your personal life for doing something that hurt someone else, but you didn't think it was a big deal at the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, not in the way you’re saying it, I don’t think. I don’t remember some big thing where I hurt somebody and thought, “Oh, that’s nothing,” and then later everybody said it was serious.\n\nAt work, I try to be careful with people. Especially with the seniors, because they’re not always steady, and you have to think about how you move them, how you talk to them, all that. So if someone says I did something wrong, I usually take that seriously, even if I don’t agree with how they’re saying it.\n\nIn my personal life… I don’t know. I’ve had arguments, like anybody. Maybe I’ve said things too harsh or came off stronger than I meant to, but I don’t know if I’d call that hurting someone and not thinking it mattered. I can get defensive if I feel like someone’s accusing me, but I don’t set out to hurt people. If you mean physically or doing something dangerous and then brushing it off, no, I can’t really think of anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your supervisor and coworkers are targeting you is something that might not be true, or do you feel absolutely certain about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if there’s a chance I’m wrong, and I guess in my head I can say, like, maybe I don’t have a paper in my hand proving it. I can’t show you a notebook where they wrote my name down or anything like that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a maybe to me. It feels pretty certain. Even when I’m calm, even when I’m not upset and I’m just doing the work normal, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple people around her are keeping track of me. Like they’re watching what I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer things, and then these little comments come right after. They’ll say something about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, and it sounds innocent if you just hear the words, but the timing of it… it feels like it’s aimed at me.\n\nSo I don’t feel absolutely certain like I’ve got hard proof, but inside I feel sure enough that it’s hard to shake. It feels like they’re testing me, trying to get a reaction or trying to build something to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that’s what bothers me, because I do care about them. I don’t think I’m imagining the way they’re acting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you consistently disregard others' feelings or needs in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it to me like that, like, “You consistently disregard people’s feelings or needs.” That sounds pretty strong. I mean, people have told me I can be defensive, or that I’m not hearing them right away when I feel accused, and I can see how maybe that comes off like I don’t care in the moment. But I don’t think I’m somebody who just ignores what other people need.\n\nAt work especially, the whole job is about paying attention to people’s needs, you know? If someone needs help getting up, or they’re uncomfortable, or they’re scared, you have to notice that. So if somebody told me I didn’t care about that, I’d have a hard time accepting it, because I do try.\n\nIn my personal life, I guess I’ve had arguments where someone felt like I wasn’t listening enough, or I was too focused on defending myself. But consistently disregarding feelings… no, I haven’t really been told that, not in that way. If there’s something specific you mean, I might be able to answer better.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others, even before the issues started at your current job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “always.” I mean, I’ve had problems getting along with certain people, sure, but I don’t think it’s been my whole life like I just can’t be around people or I’m always fighting with everybody.\n\nBefore this job, I had disagreements, like anyone does. Sometimes I don’t explain myself right, or I get frustrated if I feel like somebody’s putting words in my mouth. And I can be quiet around people if I don’t trust the situation, so maybe some people take that the wrong way. But I’ve also gotten along with people fine. I’ve had coworkers I could talk to, people I helped, people who knew I was trying to do my job.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had difficulty getting along with others. Not like this. This current job feels different to me. But if you mean have I ever had conflicts before, yeah, I mean, I’m 41, I’ve had conflicts. I just don’t think it was some pattern where everywhere I went people had a problem with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're more important or special than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like “more important than others,” because that sounds like I think I’m better than everybody, and I don’t really mean it that way. I know everybody has their role, and people have degrees and titles and all that.\n\nBut… yeah, sometimes I do get this strong feeling like I see things clearer than they do, especially when it comes to keeping the seniors safe and actually caring about them like people. Like the supervisors and the people with the badges or titles, they talk like they know everything, but then I’m watching what actually happens on the floor, and I’m thinking, you’re missing the whole point. You’re not seeing what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s not even just that I’m good at my job. It feels bigger than that sometimes. Like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was somehow picked, or meant, to be the one who understands what the senior center really needs. I know that probably sounds like I’m making myself sound special, and maybe that’s what you’re asking, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like ego. It feels like responsibility. Like I’m the one noticing the things everyone else is ignoring.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do feel that way sometimes. Not like every single thing I say is more valuable than everyone else’s, but with certain things, especially at the senior center, I do feel like I understand what’s really going on better than the people who are supposed to be in charge.\n\nLike, they have the titles, they have the paperwork, they have the meetings and all that, but I’m there with the old folks, you know? I’m seeing when somebody’s not being watched right, or when somebody’s scared, or when the way they’re doing things isn’t really caring for them like human beings. And then when I try to say something, it feels like they brush it off because I’m “just” the aide or because I don’t have the right position.\n\nSo yes, in those moments, I do feel like my opinion should carry more weight than it does. Sometimes it feels like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was meant to see it, or picked somehow to understand what the place needs in a way they don’t. I know how that sounds, like I’m saying I’m above people, but that’s not how I mean it. It feels more like… I’m the one actually paying attention, and they’re missing what’s right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special destiny or role in life that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it a “destiny” exactly, because that sounds kind of big, like I’m saying I was put on earth for some grand mission or something. But I do get that feeling sometimes, especially when it comes to the senior center and the old folks there.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be watching how things are run, and I’ll see stuff that the people with titles don’t seem to notice. And then I get this strong feeling like I understand it better than they do. Like maybe I’m there for a reason, to protect them or to make people listen. If they would just stop brushing me off and actually hear me, I feel like I could fix the whole place, or at least a lot of what’s wrong with it.\n\nI know saying that probably sounds like I think I’m special or above other people, but it doesn’t feel like that inside. It feels more like pressure, like responsibility. Like when the residents aren’t being watched right, or when somebody’s acting scared or confused and everyone else is just treating it like routine. I just don’t think it was some pattern where everywhere I went people had a problem with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're more important or special than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like “more important than others,” because that sounds like I think I’m better than everybody, and I don’t really mean it that way. I know everybody has their role, and people have degrees and titles and all that.\n\nBut… yeah, sometimes I do get this strong feeling like I see things clearer than they do, especially when it comes to keeping the seniors safe and actually caring about them like people. Like the supervisors and the people with the badges or titles, they talk like they know everything, but then I’m watching what actually happens on the floor, and I’m thinking, you’re missing the whole point. You’re not seeing what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s not even just that I’m good at my job. It feels bigger than that sometimes. Like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was somehow picked, or meant, to be the one who understands what the senior center really needs. I know that probably sounds like I’m making myself sound special, and maybe that’s what you’re asking, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like ego. It feels like responsibility. Like I’m the one noticing the things everyone else is ignoring.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do feel that way sometimes. Not like every single thing I say is more valuable than everyone else’s, but with certain things, especially at the senior center, I do feel like I understand what’s really going on better than the people who are supposed to be in charge.\n\nLike, they have the titles, they have the paperwork, they have the meetings and all that, but I’m there with the old folks, you know? I’m seeing when somebody’s not being watched right, or when somebody’s scared, or when the way they’re doing things isn’t really caring for them like human beings. And then when I try to say something, it feels like they brush it off because I’m “just” the aide or because I don’t have the right position.\n\nSo yes, in those moments, I do feel like my opinion should carry more weight than it does. Sometimes it feels like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was meant to see it, or picked somehow to understand what the place needs in a way they don’t. I know how that sounds, like I’m saying I’m above people, but that’s not how I mean it. It feels more like… I’m the one actually paying attention, and they’re missing what’s right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special destiny or role in life that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it a “destiny” exactly, because that sounds kind of big, like I’m saying I was put on earth for some grand mission or something. But I do get that feeling sometimes, especially when it comes to the senior center and the old folks there.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be watching how things are run, and I’ll see stuff that the people with titles don’t seem to notice. They’ll have their rules and clipboards and meetings, but I’m the one actually seeing the residents, seeing what they need, seeing what doesn’t feel safe or right. And then I get this strong feeling like I understand it better than they do. Like not just “I have an opinion,” but like I’m supposed to be the one to step in and fix it.\n\nSometimes it feels like I was picked for that somehow. Like maybe I’m there for a reason, to protect them or to make people listen. If they would just stop brushing me off and actually hear me, I feel like I could fix the whole place, or at least a lot of what’s wrong with it.\n\nI know saying that probably sounds like I think I’m special or above other people, but it doesn’t feel like that inside. It feels more like pressure, like responsibility. Like I’m seeing something important and everybody else is walking past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special connection or insight that others lack, which sets you apart and makes your thoughts or actions more significant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know I keep coming back to the senior center, but that’s where it comes up the strongest for me. It’s like I can walk into a situation there and just feel what’s off, you know? Like when the residents aren’t being watched right, or when somebody’s acting scared or confused and everyone else is just treating it like routine. I’ll see it, and it feels obvious to me, but the people with the titles, the supervisors, the ones who are supposed to know better, they don’t seem to catch it.\n\nAnd then I start feeling like… why am I the only one seeing this? Like there’s something about me that understands safety and caring for old folks in a way they don’t. Not because I went to some big school or have some fancy certificate, because I don’t, but because I’m actually there with them and I pay attention. I notice the little things. I know when something doesn’t sit right.\n\nSometimes it does feel like a special kind of insight, I guess. Like I was put there, or picked somehow, to fix what’s wrong in that place. Like if they would just listen to me instead of brushing me off, I could straighten a lot of it out. Maybe even the whole place, honestly. I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels real. It feels like I’m carrying something important and they’re ignoring it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about your supervisor and coworkers targeting you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say yes, because I know that’s probably the reasonable answer, right? Not just normal work notes, I mean like watching me for little mistakes, writing down how I talk to the seniors, how I react to things, whether I seem frustrated or not. And then they make these little comments that sound casual, but they don’t feel casual to me. It feels like they’re testing me, like they’re trying to see if I’ll slip up or prove I’m not safe to be around the residents.\n\nSo if you ask what would change my mind… I don’t know. If they came right out and showed me there weren’t any notes, or explained every comment, maybe that would help some. I’m not trying to use my position to benefit myself. I don’t really have that kind of power anyway. I’m just an aide. So… no, not that I can think of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of understanding and caring for the seniors is significantly better than anyone else's, to the point where you feel you have a unique role or mission?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel that way, if I’m being honest. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I know it’s possible in the way that anything is possible. Like, I’m sitting here with you, so I can say maybe my mind is making connections that other people wouldn’t make. I’m not completely closed off to that idea.

But when I’m actually at work, and even when I’m not upset — like even when I’ve gone home, cooled down, thought about it later — I still feel sure something is going on. It’s not just me having a bad moment and then realizing I overreacted. It stays with me. My supervisor and a couple of the coworkers, it feels like they’re watching me and keeping track of things, like little notes about what I say or how I talk to the seniors or whether I look irritated. And then they’ll make these small comments, like almost casual, but they land in a way that feels pointed. Like they’re testing me to see if I’ll react wrong.

So could it not be real? I can say maybe, because I know that’s what you’re asking. But it doesn’t feel like “maybe” to me most of the time. It feels like they’re trying to build a case that I’m not safe around the seniors, and I don’t know how to just talk myself out of that when the little things keep happening.

But I’ll look at the same thing and think, “No, you’re missing what’s really happening here.” I notice when somebody is scared, when they’re not being watched closely enough, when something could go wrong before it goes wrong. And then when nobody listens, it makes me feel like… why am I seeing this so clearly if I’m not supposed to do something about it?\n\nSo yeah, sometimes it feels bigger than just a job. Like writing down little things, watching how I answer, watching how I move around the residents, waiting for me to mess up. And then the comments they make — they sound normal to everybody else, I guess, but to me they feel aimed right at me. Like, “We need to make sure everyone is safe,” or “Be careful with how you handle that,” and it’s like… why say it right then, right when I’m there?\n\nSo I don’t know if they’re trying to harm me exactly, but I do think they’re trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors, like they want people to stop trusting me. And that bothers me a lot, because I care about those people. I’m not careless with them. But it feels like they’re testing me and gathering things to make me look that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these people at work are specifically targeting you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that’s how it feels to me. Not everybody at work, but my supervisor and a couple of people around her, I feel like they’re watching me different than they watch other people.\n\nEven when I’m calm, not yelling, not upset, just doing my job, I still get this strong sense that they’re keeping track of me. Like little notes, little things I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer questions. And then they make these comments that sound normal to other people, but to me they don’t feel normal. They’ll say things about being careful, or making sure residents are safe, and it comes right when I’m there, right after I did something, like they’re trying to see how I’ll react.\n\nSo yeah, I do feel specifically targeted. Like they’re trying to build something on me, trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that really bothers me because I care about them. I take that part seriously. I’m not trying to hurt anybody or be careless. But it feels like they already decided something about me and now they’re just collecting little pieces to make it look true.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that things you see or hear on TV, or read in the newspaper, are specifically about you or related to you in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really TV or newspapers, no… I don’t usually feel like the news is talking about me or anything like that. It’s more what happens at work, with people right there.\n\nLike even when I’m calm and just doing my job, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple of people there are keeping notes on me, or watching me closer than everybody else. And they’ll make these little comments that sound normal, I guess, like about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, but it feels like they’re saying it for me to hear. Like they’re testing how I react.\n\nSo it’s not like I see something on TV and think, “That’s about me.” It’s more the comments and looks at work. It feels connected to me, like they’re trying to prove I’m not safe around the seniors, and I don’t think other people see it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever gotten into trouble at work or in your personal life for doing something that hurt someone else, but you didn't think it was a big deal at the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, not in the way you’re saying it, I don’t think. I don’t remember some big thing where I hurt somebody and thought, “Oh, that’s nothing,” and then later everybody said it was serious.\n\nAt work, I try to be careful with people. Especially with the seniors, because they’re not always steady, and you have to think about how you move them, how you talk to them, all that. So if someone says I did something wrong, I usually take that seriously, even if I don’t agree with how they’re saying it.\n\nIn my personal life… I don’t know. I’ve had arguments, like anybody. Maybe I’ve said things too harsh or came off stronger than I meant to, but I don’t know if I’d call that hurting someone and not thinking it mattered. I can get defensive if I feel like someone’s accusing me, but I don’t set out to hurt people. If you mean physically or doing something dangerous and then brushing it off, no, I can’t really think of anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your belief that your supervisor and coworkers are targeting you is something that might not be true, or do you feel absolutely certain about it?\n\nPatient: I mean… I know you’re asking if there’s a chance I’m wrong, and I guess in my head I can say, like, maybe I don’t have a paper in my hand proving it. I can’t show you a notebook where they wrote my name down or anything like that.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a maybe to me. It feels pretty certain. Even when I’m calm, even when I’m not upset and I’m just doing the work normal, I still feel sure my supervisor and a couple people around her are keeping track of me. Like they’re watching what I say, how I move around the seniors, how I answer things, and then these little comments come right after. They’ll say something about being careful or making sure the seniors are safe, and it sounds innocent if you just hear the words, but the timing of it… it feels like it’s aimed at me.\n\nSo I don’t feel absolutely certain like I’ve got hard proof, but inside I feel sure enough that it’s hard to shake. It feels like they’re testing me, trying to get a reaction or trying to build something to prove I’m not safe around the seniors. And that’s what bothers me, because I do care about them. I don’t think I’m imagining the way they’re acting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that you consistently disregard others' feelings or needs in your personal or professional life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think anyone has said it to me like that, like, “You consistently disregard people’s feelings or needs.” That sounds pretty strong. I mean, people have told me I can be defensive, or that I’m not hearing them right away when I feel accused, and I can see how maybe that comes off like I don’t care in the moment. But I don’t think I’m somebody who just ignores what other people need.\n\nAt work especially, the whole job is about paying attention to people’s needs, you know? If someone needs help getting up, or they’re uncomfortable, or they’re scared, you have to notice that. So if somebody told me I didn’t care about that, I’d have a hard time accepting it, because I do try.\n\nIn my personal life, I guess I’ve had arguments where someone felt like I wasn’t listening enough, or I was too focused on defending myself. But consistently disregarding feelings… no, I haven’t really been told that, not in that way. If there’s something specific you mean, I might be able to answer better.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty getting along with others, even before the issues started at your current job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “always.” I mean, I’ve had problems getting along with certain people, sure, but I don’t think it’s been my whole life like I just can’t be around people or I’m always fighting with everybody.\n\nBefore this job, I had disagreements, like anyone does. Sometimes I don’t explain myself right, or I get frustrated if I feel like somebody’s putting words in my mouth. And I can be quiet around people if I don’t trust the situation, so maybe some people take that the wrong way. But I’ve also gotten along with people fine. I’ve had coworkers I could talk to, people I helped, people who knew I was trying to do my job.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I’ve always had difficulty getting along with others. Not like this. This current job feels different to me. But if you mean have I ever had conflicts before, yeah, I mean, I’m 41, I’ve had conflicts. I just don’t think it was some pattern where everywhere I went people had a problem with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're more important or special than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it like “more important than others,” because that sounds like I think I’m better than everybody, and I don’t really mean it that way. I know everybody has their role, and people have degrees and titles and all that.\n\nBut… yeah, sometimes I do get this strong feeling like I see things clearer than they do, especially when it comes to keeping the seniors safe and actually caring about them like people. Like the supervisors and the people with the badges or titles, they talk like they know everything, but then I’m watching what actually happens on the floor, and I’m thinking, you’re missing the whole point. You’re not seeing what I’m seeing.\n\nAnd it’s not even just that I’m good at my job. It feels bigger than that sometimes. Like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was somehow picked, or meant, to be the one who understands what the senior center really needs. I know that probably sounds like I’m making myself sound special, and maybe that’s what you’re asking, but in the moment it doesn’t feel like ego. It feels like responsibility. Like I’m the one noticing the things everyone else is ignoring.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like your ideas or opinions are more valuable than those of others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do feel that way sometimes. Not like every single thing I say is more valuable than everyone else’s, but with certain things, especially at the senior center, I do feel like I understand what’s really going on better than the people who are supposed to be in charge.\n\nLike, they have the titles, they have the paperwork, they have the meetings and all that, but I’m there with the old folks, you know? I’m seeing when somebody’s not being watched right, or when somebody’s scared, or when the way they’re doing things isn’t really caring for them like human beings. And then when I try to say something, it feels like they brush it off because I’m “just” the aide or because I don’t have the right position.\n\nSo yes, in those moments, I do feel like my opinion should carry more weight than it does. Sometimes it feels like if they would just listen to me, I could fix a lot of what’s wrong there. Like I was meant to see it, or picked somehow to understand what the place needs in a way they don’t. I know how that sounds, like I’m saying I’m above people, but that’s not how I mean it. It feels more like… I’m the one actually paying attention, and they’re missing what’s right in front of them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special destiny or role in life that sets you apart from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it a “destiny” exactly, because that sounds kind of big, like I’m saying I was put on earth for some grand mission or something. But I do get that feeling sometimes, especially when it comes to the senior center and the old folks there.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll be watching how things are run, and I’ll see stuff that the people with titles don’t seem to notice. They’ll have their rules and clipboards and meetings, but I’m the one actually seeing the residents, seeing what they need, seeing what doesn’t feel safe or right. And then I get this strong feeling like I understand it better than they do. Like not just “I have an opinion,” but like I’m supposed to be the one to step in and fix it.\n\nSometimes it feels like I was picked for that somehow. Like maybe I’m there for a reason, to protect them or to make people listen. If they would just stop brushing me off and actually hear me, I feel like I could fix the whole place, or at least a lot of what’s wrong with it.\n\nI know saying that probably sounds like I think I’m special or above other people, but it doesn’t feel like that inside. It feels more like pressure, like responsibility. Like I’m seeing something important and everybody else is walking past it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you have a special connection or insight that others lack, which sets you apart and makes your thoughts or actions more significant?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know I keep coming back to the senior center, but that’s where it comes up the strongest for me. It’s like I can walk into a situation there and just feel what’s off, you know? Like when the residents aren’t being watched right, or when somebody’s acting scared or confused and everyone else is just treating it like routine. I’ll see it, and it feels obvious to me, but the people with the titles, the supervisors, the ones who are supposed to know better, they don’t seem to catch it.\n\nAnd then I start feeling like… why am I the only one seeing this? Like there’s something about me that understands safety and caring for old folks in a way they don’t. Not because I went to some big school or have some fancy certificate, because I don’t, but because I’m actually there with them and I pay attention. I notice the little things. I know when something doesn’t sit right.\n\nSometimes it does feel like a special kind of insight, I guess. Like I was put there, or picked somehow, to fix what’s wrong in that place. Like if they would just listen to me instead of brushing me off, I could straighten a lot of it out. Maybe even the whole place, honestly. I know that sounds like a lot when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels real. It feels like I’m carrying something important and they’re ignoring it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about your supervisor and coworkers targeting you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I want to say yes, because I know that’s probably the reasonable answer, right? Like if somebody showed me something clear enough, I should be able to say, “Okay, maybe I got it wrong.” I’m not trying to be impossible.\n\nBut honestly, even when I’m calm, even when I’m not worked up or angry about it, I still feel pretty sure my supervisor and a couple people there are keeping notes on me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “special status,” because that sounds like I think I’m better than everybody or like I’m some boss, and I know I’m not. I don’t have a title. I’m not the supervisor. I’m just an aide.

But do I feel like I see things other people don’t? Yes. I really do. Especially when it comes to the seniors and keeping them safe, knowing when something isn’t right, knowing when somebody needs more attention than they’re getting. I get this strong feeling sometimes that I understand caring for old folks better than the people who are supposed to be in charge. Like they have the title, but I have the sense of what’s actually happening.

And when that feeling gets strong, it does start to feel like… maybe I’m there for a reason. Like maybe I’m the one who’s supposed to fix the whole senior center, or at least make them listen and do things the right way. Not because I want power or anything like that, but because I feel like if they would just stop brushing me off, I could help straighten it out.

So, yeah, I guess in that way I do feel like I have some kind of role or authority that other people don’t recognize. I don’t know if I’d call it “status,” but it feels important to me.
